## original-en · klopstock · 12:24

I noticed some of the company prepare their mouths and draw themselves up at the prospect of some agreeable forfeit. "Let us play at counting," said Charlotte. "Now, pay attention: I shall go round the circle from right to left; and each person is to count, one after the other, the number that comes to him, and must count fast; whoever stops or mistakes is to have a box on the ear, and so on, till we have counted a thousand." It was delightful to see the fun. She went round the circle with upraised arm. "One," said the first; "two," the second; "three," the third; and so on, till Charlotte went faster and faster. One made a mistake, instantly a box on the ear; and, amid the laughter that ensued, came another box; and so on, faster and faster. I myself came in for two. I fancied they were harder than the rest, and felt quite delighted. A general laughter and confusion put an end to the game long before we had counted as far as a thousand. The party broke up into little separate knots: the storm had ceased, and I followed Charlotte into the ballroom. On the way she said, "The game banished their fears of the storm." I could make no reply. "I myself," she continued, "was as much frightened as any of them; but by affecting courage, to keep up the spirits of the others, I forgot my apprehensions." We went to the window. It was still thundering at a distance: a soft rain was pouring down over the country, and filled the air around us with delicious odours. Charlotte leaned forward on her arm; her eyes wandered over the scene; she raised them to the sky, and then turned them upon me; they were moistened with tears; she placed her hand on mine and said, "Klopstock!" at once I remembered the magnificent ode which was in her thoughts: I felt oppressed with the weight of my sensations, and sank under them. It was more than I could bear. I bent over her hand, kissed it in a stream of delicious tears, and again looked up to her eyes. Divine Klopstock! why didst thou not see thy apotheosis in those eyes? And thy name so often profaned, would that I never heard it repeated!

The German poet whose ode Charlotte and Werther recall during the storm.

## original-en · ernesti · 37:0

If my ills would admit of any cure, they would certainly be cured here. This is my birthday, and early in the morning I received a packet from Albert. Upon opening it, I found one of the pink ribbons which Charlotte wore in her dress the first time I saw her, and which I had several times asked her to give me. With it were two volumes in duodecimo of Wetstein's "Homer," a book I had often wished for, to save me the inconvenience of carrying the large Ernestine edition with me upon my walks. You see how they anticipate my wishes, how well they understand all those little attentions of friendship, so superior to the costly presents of the great, which are humiliating. I kissed the ribbon a thousand times, and in every breath inhaled the remembrance of those happy and irrevocable days which filled me with the keenest joy. Such, Wilhelm, is our fate. I do not murmur at it: the flowers of life are but visionary. How many pass away, and leave no trace behind—how few yield any fruit—and the fruit itself, how rarely does it ripen! And yet there are flowers enough! and is it not strange, my friend, that we should suffer the little that does really ripen, to rot, decay, and perish unenjoyed? Farewell! This is a glorious summer. I often climb into the trees in Charlotte's orchard, and shake down the pears that hang on the highest branches. She stands below, and catches them as they fall.

The classical scholar associated with Werther’s larger edition of Homer.

## original-en · francis · 48:1

The Count of O— likes and distinguishes me. It is well known, and I have mentioned this to you a hundred times. Yesterday I dined with him. It is the day on which the nobility are accustomed to assemble at his house in the evening. I never once thought of the assembly, nor that we subalterns did not belong to such society. Well, I dined with the count; and, after dinner, we adjourned to the large hall. We walked up and down together: and I conversed with him, and with Colonel B—, who joined us; and in this manner the hour for the assembly approached. God knows, I was thinking of nothing, when who should enter but the honourable Lady accompanied by her noble husband and their silly, scheming daughter, with her small waist and flat neck; and, with disdainful looks and a haughty air they passed me by. As I heartily detest the whole race, I determined upon going away; and only waited till the count had disengaged himself from their impertinent prattle, to take leave, when the agreeable Miss B— came in. As I never meet her without experiencing a heartfelt pleasure, I stayed and talked to her, leaning over the back of her chair, and did not perceive, till after some time, that she seemed a little confused, and ceased to answer me with her usual ease of manner. I was struck with it. "Heavens!" I said to myself, "can she, too, be like the rest?" I felt annoyed, and was about to withdraw; but I remained, notwithstanding, forming excuses for her conduct, fancying she did not mean it, and still hoping to receive some friendly recognition. The rest of the company now arrived. There was the Baron F—, in an entire suit that dated from the coronation of Francis I.; the Chancellor N—, with his deaf wife; the shabbily-dressed I—, whose old-fashioned coat bore evidence of modern repairs: this crowned the whole. I conversed with some of my acquaintances, but they answered me laconically. I was engaged in observing Miss B—, and did not notice that the women were whispering at the end of the room, that the murmur extended by degrees to the men, that Madame S— addressed the count with much warmth (this was all related to me subsequently by Miss B—); till at length the count came up to me, and took me to the window. "You know our ridiculous customs," he said. "I perceive the company is rather displeased at your being here. I would not on any account—" "I beg your excellency's pardon!" I exclaimed. "I ought to have thought of this before, but I know you will forgive this little inattention. I was going," I added, "some time ago, but my evil genius detained me." And I smiled and bowed, to take my leave. He shook me by the hand, in a manner which expressed everything. I hastened at once from the illustrious assembly, sprang into a carriage, and drove to M—. I contemplated the setting sun from the top of the hill, and read that beautiful passage in Homer, where Ulysses is entertained by the hospitable herdsmen. This was indeed delightful.

The Holy Roman emperor whose coronation dates Baron F—’s old-fashioned suit.

## original-en · lavater · 65:0

It makes me wretched, Wilhelm, to think that there should be men incapable of appreciating the few things which possess a real value in life. You remember the walnut trees at S—, under which I used to sit with Charlotte, during my visits to the worthy old vicar. Those glorious trees, the very sight of which has so often filled my heart with joy, how they adorned and refreshed the parsonage yard, with their wide-extended branches! and how pleasing was our remembrance of the good old pastor, by whose hands they were planted so many years ago: The schoolmaster has frequently mentioned his name. He had it from his grandfather. He must have been a most excellent man; and, under the shade of those old trees, his memory was ever venerated by me. The schoolmaster informed us yesterday, with tears in his eyes, that those trees had been felled. Yes, cut to the ground! I could, in my wrath, have slain the monster who struck the first stroke. And I must endure this!—I, who, if I had had two such trees in my own court, and one had died from old age, should have wept with real affliction. But there is some comfort left, such a thing is sentiment, the whole village murmurs at the misfortune; and I hope the vicar's wife will soon find, by the cessation of the villagers' presents, how much she has wounded the feelings of the neighborhhood. It was she who did it, the wife of the present incumbent (our good old man is dead), a tall, sickly creature who is so far right to disregard the world, as the world totally disregards her. The silly being affects to be learned, pretends to examine the canonical books, lends her aid toward the new-fashioned reformation of Christendom, moral and critical, and shrugs up her shoulders at the mention of Lavater's enthusiasm. Her health is destroyed, on account of which she is prevented from having any enjoyment here below. Only such a creature could have cut down my walnut trees! I can never pardon it. Hear her reasons. The falling leaves made the court wet and dirty; the branches obstructed the light; boys threw stones at the nuts when they were ripe, and the noise affected her nerves; and disturbed her profound meditations, when she was weighing the difficulties of Kennicot, Semler, and Michaelis. Finding that all the parish, particularly the old people, were displeased, I asked "why they allowed it?" "Ah, sir!" they replied, "when the steward orders, what can we poor peasants do?" But one thing has happened well. The steward and the vicar (who, for once, thought to reap some advantage from the caprices of his wife) intended to divide the trees between them. The revenue-office, being informed of it, revived an old claim to the ground where the trees had stood, and sold them to the best bidder. There they still lie on the ground. If I were the sovereign, I should know how to deal with them all, vicar, steward, and revenue-office. Sovereign, did I say? I should, in that case, care little about the trees that grew in the country.

The Swiss religious writer mentioned in the account of the new vicar’s wife.

## original-en · melusina · 4:0

I know not whether some deceitful spirits haunt this spot, or whether it be the warm, celestial fancy in my own heart which makes everything around me seem like paradise. In front of the house is a fountain,—a fountain to which I am bound by a charm like Melusina and her sisters. Descending a gentle slope, you come to an arch, where, some twenty steps lower down, water of the clearest crystal gushes from the marble rock. The narrow wall which encloses it above, the tall trees which encircle the spot, and the coolness of the place itself,—everything imparts a pleasant but sublime impression. Not a day passes on which I do not spend an hour there. The young maidens come from the town to fetch water,—innocent and necessary employment, and formerly the occupation of the daughters of kings. As I take my rest there, the idea of the old patriarchal life is awakened around me. I see them, our old ancestors, how they formed their friendships and contracted alliances at the fountain-side; and I feel how fountains and streams were guarded by beneficent spirits. He who is a stranger to these sensations has never really enjoyed cool repose at the side of a fountain after the fatigue of a weary summer day.

The water spirit of European legend, invoked beside the fountain.

## original-en · penelope · 14:5

When, in the morning at sunrise, I go out to Walheim, and with my own hands gather in the garden the pease which are to serve for my dinner, when I sit down to shell them, and read my Homer during the intervals, and then, selecting a saucepan from the kitchen, fetch my own butter, put my mess on the fire, cover it up, and sit down to stir it as occasion requires, I figure to myself the illustrious suitors of Penelope, killing, dressing, and preparing their own oxen and swine. Nothing fills me with a more pure and genuine sense of happiness than those traits of patriarchal life which, thank Heaven! I can imitate without affectation. Happy is it, indeed, for me that my heart is capable of feeling the same simple and innocent pleasure as the peasant whose table is covered with food of his own rearing, and who not only enjoys his meal, but remembers with delight the happy days and sunny mornings when he planted it, the soft evenings when he watered it, and the pleasure he experienced in watching its daily growth.

Odysseus’s wife in Homer’s Odyssey.

## original-en · fictitious-leonora · 12:12

"When I was younger," she observed, "I loved nothing so much as romances. Nothing could equal my delight when, on some holiday, I could settle down quietly in a corner, and enter with my whole heart and soul into the joys or sorrows of some fictitious Leonora. I do not deny that they even possess some charms for me yet. But I read so seldom, that I prefer books suited exactly to my taste. And I like those authors best whose scenes describe my own situation in life,—and the friends who are about me, whose stories touch me with interest, from resembling my own homely existence,—which, without being absolutely paradise, is, on the whole, a source of indescribable happiness."

A heroine in Charlotte’s example of the stories she enjoyed as a girl.

## original-en · elijah · 20:1

I talked with Charlotte of the inconceivable manner in which men allow themselves to be blinded; how any one could avoid suspecting some deception, when seven florins only were allowed to defray expenses twice as great. But I have myself known people who believed, without any visible astonishment, that their house possessed the prophet's never-failing cruse of oil.

The biblical prophet invoked through the story of the unfailing supply of oil.

## original-en · jesus · 15:1

Yes, my dear Wilhelm, nothing on this earth affects my heart so much as children. When I look on at their doings; when I mark in the little creatures the seeds of all those virtues and qualities which they will one day find so indispensable; when I behold in the obstinate all the future firmness and constancy of a noble character; in the capricious, that levity and gaiety of temper which will carry them lightly over the dangers and troubles of life, their whole nature simple and unpolluted,—then I call to mind the golden words of the Great Teacher of mankind, "Unless ye become like one of these!" And now, my friend, these children, who are our equals, whom we ought to consider as our models, we treat them as though they were our subjects. They are allowed no will of their own. And have we, then, none ourselves? Whence comes our exclusive right? Is it because we are older and more experienced? Great God! from the height of thy heaven thou beholdest great children and little children, and no others; and thy Son has long since declared which afford thee greatest pleasure. But they believe in him, and hear him not,—that, too, is an old story; and they train their children after their own image, etc.

The Christian teacher whose words Werther recalls.

## original-en · levite · 32:5

"Oh! you people of sound understandings," I replied, smiling, "are ever ready to exclaim 'Extravagance, and madness, and intoxication!' You moral men are so calm and so subdued! You abhor the drunken man, and detest the extravagant; you pass by, like the Levite, and thank God, like the Pharisee, that you are not like one of them. I have been more than once intoxicated, my passions have always bordered on extravagance: I am not ashamed to confess it; for I have learned, by my own experience, that all extraordinary men, who have accomplished great and astonishing actions, have ever been decried by the world as drunken or insane. And in private life, too, is it not intolerable that no one can undertake the execution of a noble or generous deed, without giving rise to the exclamation that the doer is intoxicated or mad? Shame upon you, ye sages!"

The passerby from the Good Samaritan parable, used as a comparison.

## original-en · pharisee · 32:5

You abhor the drunken man, and detest the extravagant; you pass by, like the Levite, and thank God, like the Pharisee, that you are not like one of them. I have been more than once intoxicated, my passions have always bordered on extravagance: I am not ashamed to confess it; for I have learned, by my own experience, that all extraordinary men, who have accomplished great and astonishing actions, have ever been decried by the world as drunken or insane. And in private life, too, is it not intolerable that no one can undertake the execution of a noble or generous deed, without giving rise to the exclamation that the doer is intoxicated or mad? Shame upon you, ye sages!"

The self-satisfied worshipper from Jesus’s parable, used in Werther’s argument.

## original-en · samaritan · 84:109

"I have implored your father to protect my remains. At the corner of the churchyard, looking toward the fields, there are two lime-trees—there I wish to lie. Your father can, and doubtless will, do this much for his friend. Implore it of him. But perhaps pious Christians will not choose that their bodies should be buried near the corpse of a poor, unhappy wretch like me. Then let me be laid in some remote valley, or near the highway, where the priest and Levite may bless themselves as they pass by my tomb, whilst the Samaritan will shed a tear for my fate.

The compassionate traveler of Jesus’s parable.

## original-en · god · 2:0

How happy I am that I am gone! My dear friend, what a thing is the heart of man! To leave you, from whom I have been inseparable, whom I love so dearly, and yet to feel happy! I know you will forgive me. Have not other attachments been specially appointed by fate to torment a head like mine? Poor Leonora! and yet I was not to blame. Was it my fault, that, whilst the peculiar charms of her sister afforded me an agreeable entertainment, a passion for me was engendered in her feeble heart? And yet am I wholly blameless? Did I not encourage her emotions? Did I not feel charmed at those truly genuine expressions of nature, which, though but little mirthful in reality, so often amused us? Did I not—but oh! what is man, that he dares so to accuse himself? My dear friend I promise you I will improve; I will no longer, as has ever been my habit, continue to ruminate on every petty vexation which fortune may dispense; I will enjoy the present, and the past shall be for me the past. No doubt you are right, my best of friends, there would be far less suffering amongst mankind, if men—and God knows why they are so fashioned—did not employ their imaginations so assiduously in recalling the memory of past sorrow, instead of bearing their present lot with equanimity. Be kind enough to inform my mother that I shall attend to her business to the best of my ability, and shall give her the earliest information about it. I have seen my aunt, and find that she is very far from being the disagreeable person our friends allege her to be. She is a lively, cheerful woman, with the best of hearts. I explained to her my mother's wrongs with regard to that part of her portion which has been withheld from her. She told me the motives and reasons of her own conduct, and the terms on which she is willing to give up the whole, and to do more than we have asked. In short, I cannot write further upon this subject at present; only assure my mother that all will go on well. And I have again observed, my dear friend, in this trifling affair, that misunderstandings and neglect occasion more mischief in the world than even malice and wickedness. At all events, the two latter are of less frequent occurrence.

The divine being Werther and others address in prayer and reflection.

## original-en · ossian · 19:0

You should see how foolish I look in company when her name is mentioned, particularly when I am asked plainly how I like her. How I like her! I detest the phrase. What sort of creature must he be who merely liked Charlotte, whose whole heart and senses were not entirely absorbed by her. Like her! Some one asked me lately how I liked Ossian.

The legendary bard in the poems Werther reads and translates.

## original-en · fingal · 67:0

Ossian has superseded Homer in my heart. To what a world does the illustrious bard carry me! To wander over pathless wilds, surrounded by impetuous whirlwinds, where, by the feeble light of the moon, we see the spirits of our ancestors; to hear from the mountain-tops, mid the roar of torrents, their plaintive sounds issuing from deep caverns, and the sorrowful lamentations of a maiden who sighs and expires on the mossy tomb of the warrior by whom she was adored. I meet this bard with silver hair; he wanders in the valley; he seeks the footsteps of his fathers, and, alas! he finds only their tombs. Then, contemplating the pale moon, as she sinks beneath the waves of the rolling sea, the memory of bygone days strikes the mind of the hero, days when approaching danger invigorated the brave, and the moon shone upon his bark laden with spoils, and returning in triumph. When I read in his countenance deep sorrow, when I see his dying glory sink exhausted into the grave, as he inhales new and heart-thrilling delight from his approaching union with his beloved, and he casts a look on the cold earth and the tall grass which is so soon to cover him, and then exclaims, "The traveller will come,—he will come who has seen my beauty, and he will ask, 'Where is the bard, where is the illustrious son of Fingal?' He will walk over my tomb, and will seek me in vain!" Then, O my friend, I could instantly, like a true and noble knight, draw my sword, and deliver my prince from the long and painful languor of a living death, and dismiss my own soul to follow the demigod whom my hand had set free!

The heroic king in the Ossian poems.

## original-en · ullin · 84:60

"And it does arise in its strength! I behold my departed friends. Their gathering is on Lora, as in the days of other years. How are ye changed, my friends, since the days of Selma's feast! when we contended, like gales of spring as they fly along the hill, and bend by turns the feebly whistling grass.

## original-en · colma · 84:61

"Minona came forth in her beauty, with downcast look and tearful eye. Her hair was flying slowly with the blast that rushed unfrequent from the hill. ## original-en · colma-brother · 84:63

"Rise moon! from behind thy clouds. Stars of the night, arise! Lead me, some light, to the place where my love rests from the chase alone! His bow near him unstrung, his dogs panting around him! But here I must sit alone by the rock of the mossy stream. The stream and the wind roar aloud. I hear not the voice of my love! Why delays my Salgar; why the chief of the hill his promise? Here is the rock and here the tree! here is the roaring stream! Thou didst promise with night to be here. Ah! whither is my Salgar gone? With thee I would fly from my father, with thee from my brother of pride. Our race have long been foes: we are not foes, O Salgar!

The brother opposed to Colma’s love for Salgar.

## original-en · torman · 84:67

"Such was thy song, Minona, softly blushing daughter of Torman. Our tears descended for Colma, and our souls were sad! Ullin came with his harp; he gave the song of Alpin. The voice of Alpin was pleasant, the soul of Ryno was a beam of fire! But they had rested in the narrow house: their voice had ceased in Selma! Ullin had returned one day from the chase before the heroes fell. He heard their strife on the hill: their song was soft, but sad! They mourned the fall of Morar, first of mortal men! His soul was like the soul of Fingal: his sword like the sword of Oscar. But he fell, and his father mourned: his sister's eyes were full of tears. Minona's eyes were full of tears, the sister of car-borne Morar. She retired from the song of Ullin, like the moon in the west, when she foresees the shower, and hides her fair head in a cloud. I touched the harp with Ullin: the song of morning rose!

The father of Minona and Morar in the Ossian poems.

## original-en · morar-mother · 84:71

"Narrow is thy dwelling now! dark the place of thine abode! With three steps I compass thy grave, O thou who wast so great before! Four stones, with their heads of moss, are the only memorial of thee. A tree with scarce a leaf, long grass which whistles in the wind, mark to the hunter's eye the grave of the mighty Morar. Morar! thou art low indeed. Thou hast no mother to mourn thee, no maid with her tears of love. Dead is she that brought thee forth. Fallen is the daughter of Morglan.

The mother mentioned in the lament for Morar.

## original-en · armin · 84:73

"The grief of all arose, but most the bursting sigh of Armin. He remembers the death of his son, who fell in the days of his youth. Carmor was near the hero, the chief of the echoing Galmal. Why burst the sigh of Armin? he said. Is there a cause to mourn? The song comes with its music to melt and please the soul. It is like soft mist that, rising from a lake, pours on the silent vale; the green flowers are filled with dew, but the sun returns in his strength, and the mist is gone. Why art thou sad, O Armin, chief of sea-surrounded Gorma?

The chief of Gorma and father of Daura and Arindal in the Ossian song.

## original-en · arindal · 84:75

"Arise, winds of autumn, arise: blow along the heath. Streams of the mountains, roar; roar, tempests in the groves of my oaks! Walk through broken clouds, O moon! show thy pale face at intervals; bring to my mind the night when all my children fell, when Arindal the mighty fell—when Daura the lovely failed. Daura, my daughter, thou wert fair, fair as the moon on Fura, white as the driven snow, sweet as the breathing gale. Arindal, thy bow was strong, thy spear was swift on the field, thy look was like mist on the wave, thy shield a red cloud in a storm! Armar, renowned in war, came and sought Daura's love. He was not long refused: fair was the hope of their friends.

Armin’s son and Daura’s brother.

## original-en · erath · 84:76

"Erath, son of Odgal, repined: his brother had been slain by Armar. He came disguised like a son of the sea: fair was his cliff on the wave, white his locks of age, calm his serious brow. Fairest of women, he said, lovely daughter of Armin! a rock not distant in the sea bears a tree on its side; red shines the fruit afar. There Armar waits for Daura. I come to carry his love! she went she called on Armar. Nought answered, but the son of the rock. Armar, my love, my love! why tormentest thou me with fear? Hear, son of Arnart, hear! it is Daura who calleth thee. Erath, the traitor, fled laughing to the land. She lifted up her voice—she called for her brother and her father. Arindal! Armin! none to relieve you, Daura.

The son of Odgal whose grievance against Armar enters the song of Daura.

## original-en · werther-father · 51:0

I leave this place to-morrow; and, as my native place is only six miles from the high road, I intend to visit it once more, and recall the happy dreams of my childhood. I shall enter at the same gate through which I came with my mother, when, after my father's death, she left that delightful retreat to immure herself in your melancholy town. Adieu, my dear friend: you shall hear of my future career.

Werther’s late father, remembered in the account of leaving his childhood home.

## original-en · albert-father · 12:7

I mentioned to you the other day that I had become acquainted with S—, the district judge, and that he had invited me to go and visit him in his retirement, or rather in his little kingdom. But I neglected going, and perhaps should never have gone, if chance had not discovered to me the treasure which lay concealed in that retired spot. Some of our young people had proposed giving a ball in the country, at which I consented to be present. I offered my hand for the evening to a pretty and agreeable, but rather commonplace, sort of girl from the immediate neighbourhood; and it was agreed that I should engage a carriage, and call upon Charlotte, with my partner and her aunt, to convey them to the ball. My companion informed me, as we drove along through the park to the hunting-lodge, that I should make the acquaintance of a very charming young lady. "Take care," added the aunt, "that you do not lose your heart." "Why?" said I. "Because she is already engaged to a very worthy man," she replied, "who is gone to settle his affairs upon the death of his father, and will succeed to a very considerable inheritance." This information possessed no interest for me. When we arrived at the gate, the sun was setting behind the tops of the mountains. The atmosphere was heavy; and the ladies expressed their fears of an approaching storm, as masses of low black clouds were gathering in the horizon. I relieved their anxieties by pretending to be weather-wise, although I myself had some apprehensions lest our pleasure should be interrupted.

Albert’s late father, whose affairs Albert has gone to settle.

## original-en · henry-father · 80:1

About dinner-time I went to walk by the river-side, for I had no appetite. Everything around seemed gloomy: a cold and damp easterly wind blew from the mountains, and black, heavy clouds spread over the plain. I observed at a distance a man in a tattered coat: he was wandering among the rocks, and seemed to be looking for plants. When I approached, he turned round at the noise; and I saw that he had an interesting countenance in which a settled melancholy, strongly marked by benevolence, formed the principal feature. His long black hair was divided, and flowed over his shoulders. As his garb betokened a person of the lower order, I thought he would not take it ill if I inquired about his business; and I therefore asked what he was seeking. He replied, with a deep sigh, that he was looking for flowers, and could find none. "But it is not the season," I observed, with a smile. "Oh, there are so many flowers!" he answered, as he came nearer to me. "In my garden there are roses and honeysuckles of two sorts: one sort was given to me by my father! they grow as plentifully as weeds; I have been looking for them these two days, and cannot find them. There are flowers out there, yellow, blue, and red; and that centaury has a very pretty blossom: but I can find none of them." I observed his peculiarity, and therefore asked him, with an air of indifference, what he intended to do with his flowers. A strange smile overspread his countenance. Holding his finger to his mouth, he expressed a hope that I would not betray him; and he then informed me that he had promised to gather a nosegay for his mistress. "That is right," said I. "Oh!" he replied, "she possesses many other things as well: she is very rich." "And yet," I continued, "she likes your nosegays." "Oh, she has jewels and crowns!" he exclaimed. I asked who she was. "If the states-general would but pay me," he added, "I should be quite another man. Alas! there was a time when I was so happy; but that is past, and I am now—" He raised his swimming eyes to heaven. "And you were happy once?" I observed. "Ah, would I were so still!" was his reply. "I was then as gay and contented as a man can be." An old woman, who was coming toward us, now called out, "Henry, Henry! where are you? We have been looking for you everywhere: come to dinner." "Is he your son?" I inquired, as I went toward her. "Yes," she said: "he is my poor, unfortunate son. The Lord has sent me a heavy affliction." I asked whether he had been long in this state. She answered, "He has been as calm as he is at present for about six months. I thank Heaven that he has so far recovered: he was for one whole year quite raving, and chained down in a madhouse. Now he injures no one, but talks of nothing else than kings and queens. He used to be a very good, quiet youth, and helped to maintain me; he wrote a very fine hand; but all at once he became melancholy, was seized with a violent fever, grew distracted, and is now as you see. If I were only to tell you, sir—" I interrupted her by asking what period it was in which he boasted of having been so happy. "Poor boy!" she exclaimed, with a smile of compassion, "he means the time when he was completely deranged, a time he never ceases to regret, when he was in the madhouse, and unconscious of everything." I was thunderstruck: I placed a piece of money in her hand, and hastened away.

The father Henry mentions while talking about flowers.

## original-en · well-girl · 6:2

The other day I went to the fountain, and found a young servant-girl, who had set her pitcher on the lowest step, and looked around to see if one of her companions was approaching to place it on her head. I ran down, and looked at her. "Shall I help you, pretty lass?" said I. She blushed deeply. "Oh, sir!" she exclaimed. "No ceremony!" I replied. She adjusted her head-gear, and I helped her. She thanked me, and ascended the steps.

The servant girl whose pitcher Werther helps lift.

## original-en · wetstein · 37:0

If my ills would admit of any cure, they would certainly be cured here. This is my birthday, and early in the morning I received a packet from Albert. Upon opening it, I found one of the pink ribbons which Charlotte wore in her dress the first time I saw her, and which I had several times asked her to give me. With it were two volumes in duodecimo of Wetstein's "Homer," a book I had often wished for, to save me the inconvenience of carrying the large Ernestine edition with me upon my walks. You see how they anticipate my wishes, how well they understand all those little attentions of friendship, so superior to the costly presents of the great, which are humiliating. I kissed the ribbon a thousand times, and in every breath inhaled the remembrance of those happy and irrevocable days which filled me with the keenest joy. Such, Wilhelm, is our fate. I do not murmur at it: the flowers of life are but visionary. How many pass away, and leave no trace behind—how few yield any fruit—and the fruit itself, how rarely does it ripen! And yet there are flowers enough! and is it not strange, my friend, that we should suffer the little that does really ripen, to rot, decay, and perish unenjoyed? Farewell! This is a glorious summer. I often climb into the trees in Charlotte's orchard, and shake down the pears that hang on the highest branches. She stands below, and catches them as they fall.

The publishing name on the small edition of Homer given to Werther.

## original-en · w · 18:0

What a child is man that he should be so solicitous about a look! What a child is man! We had been to Walheim: the ladies went in a carriage; but during our walk I thought I saw in Charlotte's dark eyes—I am a fool—but forgive me! you should see them,—those eyes.—However, to be brief (for my own eyes are weighed down with sleep), you must know, when the ladies stepped into their carriage again, young W. Seldstadt, Andran, and I were standing about the door. They are a merry set of fellows, and they were all laughing and joking together. I watched Charlotte's eyes. They wandered from one to the other; but they did not light on me, on me, who stood there motionless, and who saw nothing but her! My heart bade her a thousand times adieu, but she noticed me not. The carriage drove off; and my eyes filled with tears. I looked after her: suddenly I saw Charlotte's bonnet leaning out of the window, and she turned to look back, was it at me? My dear friend, I know not; and in this uncertainty I find consolation. Perhaps she turned to look at me. Perhaps! Good-night—what a child I am!

The young man standing with the company as Charlotte’s carriage departs.

## modern-en · werther · 1:0

I have gathered with care everything I could learn about the story of poor Werther, and I set it before you here, certain that you will thank me for it. You cannot withhold your admiration and love from his spirit and character, nor your tears from his fate.

## modern-en · charlotte · 7:4

I have also got to know a very decent man, the district steward — a frank, warm-hearted soul. They tell me it is a real joy to see him among his children; he has nine, and his eldest daughter especially is much spoken of. He has invited me to visit, and I mean to go at the first chance. He lives at one of the prince's hunting lodges, an hour and a half's walk from here, which he was given leave to move into after his wife's death, since living in town and at the court was too painful for him.

The district judge’s eldest daughter, who cares for her younger siblings and is engaged to Albert.

## modern-en · albert · 12:7

I mentioned the other day that I'd got to know S—, the district steward, and that he'd invited me out to see him in his retreat — his little kingdom, rather. I put it off, and might never have gone if chance hadn't shown me the treasure hidden away in that quiet spot. Some of our young people had got up a country ball, and I agreed to go. I offered to escort a pretty, pleasant, but rather ordinary girl from the neighborhood, and it was settled that I'd hire a carriage and drive out with my partner and her aunt to collect Charlotte on the way. "You'll meet a very lovely young woman," my companion told me as we drove through the woods toward the hunting lodge. "Take care you don't lose your heart," the aunt added. "Why?" I asked. "Because she's already promised to a very fine man," she said, "who's away just now settling his affairs after his father's death, and stands to come into a sizable inheritance." The news meant nothing to me. As we reached the gate the sun was just going down behind the mountaintops. The air was heavy, and the ladies fretted about a storm coming on, with banks of low black cloud massing on the horizon. I soothed them by playing the weather prophet, though I had my own misgivings that our evening might be spoiled.

Charlotte’s fiancé.

## modern-en · wilhelm · 2:0

How glad I am to be away! Dear friend, what a strange thing the human heart is! To leave you — you whom I have been inseparable from, whom I love so dearly — and still to feel happy! I know you will forgive me. Weren't my other attachments practically appointed by fate to torment a mind like mine? Poor Leonora! And yet I was not to blame. Was it my fault that, while her sister's particular charms gave me such pleasant company, a passion took root in her own fragile heart? And yet — am I entirely innocent? Didn't I encourage her feelings? Didn't I delight in those wholly genuine expressions of nature that, though hardly comic in themselves, so often made us laugh? Didn't I — oh, what is a man, that he dares accuse himself like this? I promise you, dear friend, I will do better; I will no longer chew over every small misfortune that fate deals out, the way I always have; I will enjoy the present, and let the past stay past. You are right, of course, my best of friends — there would be far less suffering among us if people didn't work their imaginations so hard recalling old griefs, instead of bearing the present with some composure, though God knows why we are made this way. Please tell my mother I will handle her business as well as I can and send her word of it as soon as possible. I have seen my aunt, and she is nothing like the disagreeable woman our relatives make her out to be. She is lively and warm, with the kindest heart. I explained my mother's grievance about the part of her share that has been kept back. My aunt told me her own reasons and motives, and the terms on which she is willing to hand over the whole of it — and to do more than we asked. I can't write any more about it just now; only assure my mother that it will all come right. And once again, dear friend, this small affair has shown me that misunderstanding and neglect cause more trouble in the world than even malice and cruelty do. At least the last two are rarer.

Werther’s close friend and the principal recipient of his letters.

## modern-en · count-m · 2:2

The town itself is unpleasant, but all around it lies an indescribable natural beauty. That is what moved the late Count M to lay out a garden on one of the sloping hills that cross each other here in the loveliest variety, forming the most charming valleys. The garden is simple, and the moment you enter you can tell it was not designed by a trained gardener but by a man who wanted a place to give himself up to his own feeling heart. I have already shed many a tear for its dead master in a summer-house that is now falling to ruin — his favorite spot, and now mine. Soon I will be master of the place myself. The gardener has taken to me these last few days, and he won't be the worse for it.

The late owner of the garden Werther enjoys.

## modern-en · early-friend · 7:2

How sad that the friend of my youth is gone; how sad that I ever knew her! I could tell myself: you are a fool to look for what can't be found here on earth. But she was mine. I held that heart, that noble soul, and in her presence I seemed to be more than I was, because I was everything I could be. Good God — was a single power of my soul left unused? Couldn't I unfold, in front of her, the whole of that mysterious feeling with which my heart takes in nature? Wasn't our being together an endless weave of the finest feeling and the sharpest wit, whose every variation, even at its most playful, bore the mark of genius? And now — the few years she had over me carried her to the grave before me. I will never forget her steady mind, her heavenly patience.

The older woman whose friendship Werther remembers with affection.

## modern-en · v · 7:3

A few days ago I met a young man named V—, a frank, open fellow with a very pleasant face. He has just come from the university; he doesn't think himself especially wise, but he does believe he knows more than other people. He has worked hard, as I can tell in all sorts of ways — in short, he is well informed. When he heard that I draw a good deal and that I know Greek (two rare things in this part of the country), he sought me out and unloaded his whole stock of learning, from Batteux to Wood, from De Piles to Winckelmann; he assured me he had read all the way through the first part of Sulzer's theory and owned a manuscript of Heyne's work on the study of antiquity. I let it all pass.

The young university graduate who displays his learning to Werther.

## modern-en · innkeeper · 9:1

About a league from the town there is a place called Walheim. (The reader needn't bother looking for the place under that name; we have had to change the real names given in the original.) It sits beautifully on the side of a hill, and if you follow one of the footpaths out of the village, you get a view of the whole valley. A good old woman lives there and keeps a little inn; she sells wine, beer, and coffee, and for all her age she is cheerful and pleasant. The real charm of the place is two linden trees that spread their huge branches over the small green in front of the church, which is ringed all around by peasants' cottages, barns, and farmyards. I have rarely seen anywhere so quiet and out of the way, and I often have my table and chair brought out from the inn and drink my coffee there and read my Homer. I happened on the spot one fine afternoon and found it completely deserted; everyone was out in the fields except a little boy of about four, who was sitting on the ground holding a baby of about six months between his knees, pressing it to his chest with both arms so that they made a kind of armchair. For all the liveliness sparkling in its dark eyes, the baby sat perfectly still. The sight charmed me. I sat down on a plough opposite and sketched this little picture of brotherly tenderness with real delight. I added the hedge nearby, the barn door, and a few broken cartwheels, just as they lay; and after about an hour I found I had made a well-composed, interesting drawing without putting in anything of my own. That confirmed me in my resolve to keep, from now on, entirely to nature. Nature alone is inexhaustible, and nature alone forms great masters. You can say a great deal in favor of rules — much the same as can be said for the conventions of society: a man trained on them will never produce anything downright bad or offensive, just as a man who keeps the law and minds his manners can never be an utterly unbearable neighbor or an outright villain. But say what you like, rules destroy the true feeling of nature and the true expression of it. You'll tell me that's too severe — that rules only restrain, only prune away rank growth, and so on. My good friend, let me give you an analogy. It is just like love. A warm-hearted young man gives himself wholly to a girl, spends every hour of the day with her, wears out his health and pours out his money to prove again and again that he is utterly devoted to her. Then along comes some man of the world, some man of office and standing, and says to him: "My dear young fellow, love is natural — but you must love within limits. Divide up your time: give part of it to your work and save your free hours for your sweetheart. Reckon up your money, and from whatever is left over you may make her a present — only not too often: on her birthday, say, and the like." If the young man takes this advice, he'll make a useful member of society, and I'd urge any prince to give him a post — but it's all over with his love, and, if he's an artist, with his art. Oh, my friend! Why is it that the torrent of genius so seldom breaks loose, so seldom comes roaring down in a full flood to shake your astonished soul? Because along both banks of the stream live cool, respectable people whose summer-houses and tulip beds would be ruined by it, and who therefore dig their channels and throw up their dikes in good time to head off the danger.

The older woman who runs the small inn beneath the linden trees.

## modern-en · eldest-boy · 58:0

I'm not the only one it goes so with. All men are cheated in their hopes, deceived in their expectations. I went to see my good woman under the lindens. The eldest boy ran to meet me; his shout of joy brought out his mother, and she looked very downcast. Her first word was: "Good sir, my little Hans is gone, alas!" — the youngest of her boys. I said nothing. "And my husband," she said, "is back from Switzerland with nothing; if some good people hadn't helped him, he'd have had to beg his way home. He caught a fever on the road." I could say nothing, and gave the little one something; she asked me to take a few apples, which I did, and left the place with a heavy heart.

The schoolmaster’s daughter’s eldest son, brother of Philip and Hans.

## modern-en · boys-mother · 10:0

I see I have fallen into raptures and grand talk and similes, and so forgotten to tell you what became of the children. Wrapped up in my artistic musings, which I sketched out for you yesterday, I went on sitting on that plough for a good two hours. Toward evening a young woman came hurrying toward the children, who hadn't stirred the whole time, with a basket on her arm, calling out from a distance, "Philip, you've been such a good boy!" She greeted me; I returned it, got up, and went over. I asked whether she was the mother of these pretty children. "Yes," she said, and giving the older one a piece of bread, she picked up the baby and kissed it with all a mother's tenderness. "I left my little one in Philip's care," she said, "while I went into town with my eldest to buy some white bread and sugar and an earthenware pot." I could see all of it in the basket, the lid having slipped off. "I want to make some broth tonight for my little Hans" — the baby — "but that wild one, the eldest, smashed my pot yesterday squabbling with Philip over the scrapings." I asked after the eldest, and she had barely told me he was driving a couple of geese home from the meadow when he came running up and handed Philip a willow switch. I talked with the woman a while longer and learned that she was the schoolmaster's daughter, and that her husband had gone off to Switzerland to collect a legacy left him by a relative. "They tried to cheat him out of it," she said, "and wouldn't answer his letters, so he's gone there himself. I only hope nothing has happened to him; I've heard nothing since he left." I found it hard to leave the woman; I gave each of the children a kreutzer, and one more for the baby, so she could bring it some white bread for its broth next time she went to town, and we parted. I tell you, dear friend, when my thoughts are all in an uproar, the sight of a creature like this settles my troubled mind. She moves contentedly, without a care, around the small circle of her life, getting through one day at a time; and when she sees the leaves fall it means nothing to her but that winter is coming. Since then I've gone out there often. The children have grown quite used to me; they get sugar when I drink my coffee, and in the evening they share my bread and butter and milk. On Sundays they always get their kreutzer, for the good woman has orders to hand it to them when I'm not there after the evening service. They're entirely at ease with me and tell me everything, and I get special pleasure from watching their little tempers and the simplicity of their ways when a few of the other village children are gathered with them.

The schoolmaster’s daughter, whose three boys befriend Werther at Walheim.

## modern-en · peasant · 11:1

If after that opening you're expecting something grand or magnificent, you'll be badly let down. It's nothing more than a peasant lad who has stirred the warmest interest in me. As usual I'll tell it badly, and as usual you'll think I'm overdoing it. It's Walheim again — always Walheim — that produces these wonders.

The young farm servant who is in love with the widow he works for.

## modern-en · widow · 11:3

A peasant came out of a nearby house and set about repairing part of the same plough I'd sketched a little while ago. I liked the look of him, so I spoke to him, asked about his situation, and we were soon acquainted — and, as usually happens with people of that class, he was soon confiding in me. He told me he was working for a young widow who thought very highly of him. He talked so much about his mistress and praised her so extravagantly that I could see at once he was head over heels in love with her. "She's no longer young," he said, "and her first husband treated her so badly that she has no intention of marrying again." From everything he said it was so plain what matchless charms she had for him, and how desperately he wished she would choose him to wipe out the memory of her first husband's cruelty, that I'd have to repeat his very words to give you any idea of the poor fellow's devotion, sincerity, and longing. I'd need a great poet's gifts to convey the play of his features, the music of his voice, the secret fire in his eyes. No words can capture the tenderness in his every movement and look; anything I might say would fall short. What touched me most was his anxiety that I might think badly of how things stood between him and his mistress, or doubt her good conduct. The charming way he described her figure and her person — which, though without the graces of youth, had drawn him to her and bound him fast — I can't reproduce; you'll have to imagine it. In all my life I've never seen, never pictured, never even thought possible, such intense devotion, such ardent longing, joined with so much purity. Don't blame me when I say that the memory of this innocence and truth has sunk deep into my soul, that this image of faithfulness and tenderness follows me everywhere, and that my own heart, as if kindled by the same flame, glows and burns within me.

The woman who employs the peasant lad and is the object of his love.

## modern-en · widow-brother · 61:2

Reading this letter over, I see I've forgotten to tell the end of the story — but it's easily guessed. She held herself off from him; her brother stepped in, a man who'd long hated him and wanted him out of the house, afraid that a new marriage of his sister's would rob his own children of the inheritance they hope for from her, since she's childless; and this brother threw him out at last, and made such a noise about the whole thing that the woman couldn't have taken him back even if she'd wanted to. Now she's hired another man, and they say she's fallen out with her brother over him too, and people are sure she'll marry this one — and my lad swears he won't live to see it.

The brother concerned about his children’s inheritance if the widow remarries.

## modern-en · replacement · 84:10

He stepped into the doorway, asked after the old man and Charlotte, and found the house in some commotion. The eldest boy told him a dreadful thing had happened at Walheim — a peasant had been killed! It made no special impression on him. He went in and found Charlotte busy talking the old man out of going himself, ill as he was, to look into the matter on the spot. The murderer was still unknown; the dead man had been found that morning at his own door. There were guesses: the victim had been a widow's hired man, who'd had another in the place before him, one who'd been turned out of the house with bad feeling.

The servant hired by the widow after the peasant lad’s dismissal.

## modern-en · partner · 12:7

I mentioned the other day that I'd got to know S—, the district steward, and that he'd invited me out to see him in his retreat — his little kingdom, rather. I put it off, and might never have gone if chance hadn't shown me the treasure hidden away in that quiet spot. Some of our young people had got up a country ball, and I agreed to go. I offered to escort a pretty, pleasant, but rather ordinary girl from the neighborhood, and it was settled that I'd hire a carriage and drive out with my partner and her aunt to collect Charlotte on the way. "You'll meet a very lovely young woman," my companion told me as we drove through the woods toward the hunting lodge. "Take care you don't lose your heart," the aunt added. "Why?" I asked. "Because she's already promised to a very fine man," she said, "who's away just now settling his affairs after his father's death, and stands to come into a sizable inheritance." The news meant nothing to me. As we reached the gate the sun was just going down behind the mountaintops. The air was heavy, and the ladies fretted about a storm coming on, with banks of low black cloud massing on the horizon. I soothed them by playing the weather prophet, though I had my own misgivings that our evening might be spoiled.

The young woman Werther escorts to the country ball.

## modern-en · maid · 12:8

I got down, and a maid came to the door and asked us to wait a moment for her mistress. I crossed the courtyard to the handsome house, went up the front steps, and as I opened the door I saw the most charming sight I have ever laid eyes on. Six children, from eleven years old down to two, were rushing about the hall around a girl of middle height with a lovely figure, in a plain white dress trimmed with pink ribbons. She held a loaf of dark bread and was cutting slices for the little ones around her, each according to its age and appetite, handing them out with such grace and affection, every child waiting its turn with hands outstretched and shouting out its thanks. Some ran off at once with their supper, while the gentler ones walked out to the gate to look at the strangers and the carriage that was to carry their Charlotte away. "Do forgive me for making you come in for me, and for keeping the ladies waiting," she said. "What with dressing and arranging a few household things before I go out, I forgot to give the children their supper, and they won't take it from anyone but me." I made some empty compliment, but my whole soul was lost in her bearing, her voice, her way of moving, and I had barely collected myself when she darted into her room for her gloves and fan. The children eyed me from a little way off, and I went over to the youngest, the most delightful little creature. He drew back, and just then Charlotte came in and said, "Louis, give your cousin your hand." The little fellow did so readily, and I couldn't help giving him a hearty kiss in spite of his runny nose. "Cousin?" I said to Charlotte as I handed her down. "Do you think I deserve the good fortune of being related to you?" "Oh," she answered with an easy smile, "I have such a crowd of cousins that I'd be sorry to think you the least deserving of them." As she left she asked her next sister, Sophy, a girl of about eleven, to keep a close eye on the children and to say goodbye to their father for her when he got back from his ride. She told the little ones to mind their sister Sophy as if she were Charlotte herself, and some of them promised they would; but one fair-haired little girl of about six looked unhappy and said, "But Sophy isn't you, Charlotte, and we like you best." The two eldest boys had scrambled up onto the carriage, and at my request she let them ride a little way with us through the wood, on their promise to sit quite still and hold on tight.

The maid who admits the visitors at Charlotte’s family home.

## modern-en · jane · 17:0

She's still with her dying friend, and still the same bright, lovely creature who eases pain and scatters happiness wherever she turns. Yesterday evening she went walking with her little sisters; I knew it, met them, and we went on together. After about an hour and a half we turned back toward town. We stopped at the spring I love so much — now a thousand times dearer to me than before. Charlotte sat down on the low wall and we gathered round her. I looked about me, and the time when my heart was still free and empty came back to me. "Dear spring," I said, "since then I've never once come to rest by your cool water; sometimes, hurrying past, I haven't even glanced at you." I looked down and saw Charlotte's little sister Jane coming up the steps with a glass of water. I turned to Charlotte, and felt all over again what she is to me. Just then Jane came up with the glass, and her sister Marianne tried to take it from her. "No!" the child cried, with the sweetest look, "no — Charlotte must drink first!"

One of Charlotte’s younger sisters.

Two gentlemen named Audran and a certain N.N. — I can't be bothered with all the names — who were the aunt's and Charlotte's partners, met us at the carriage door and carried off their ladies, while I led mine upstairs.

The gentlemen named Andran among the dancing company.

## modern-en · nn · 12:16

The gentleman identified only by initials among the party’s dance partners.

## modern-en · warning-lady · 12:21

In the third country dance we were the second couple. As we were going down the line — and heaven knows with what rapture I hung on her arm and her eyes, brimming with the purest, frankest delight — we passed a woman whose lovely, kindly face had caught my eye, though she was no longer young. She looked at Charlotte with a smile, raised a warning finger, and as we flew past said the name "Albert" twice, with great meaning.

The woman who pointedly reminds Charlotte of Albert during the dance.

## modern-en · old-vicar · 16:0

The comfort Charlotte can bring to a sick friend, I feel in my own heart, which suffers more from being apart from her than many a poor soul wasting away in bed. She's spending a few days in town with a worthy woman whom the doctors have given up, and who wants Charlotte near her at the end. Last week I went with her to visit the vicar of St—, a little village up in the hills about a league from here. We got there around four; Charlotte had brought her little sister along. As we came into the vicarage yard we found the good old man sitting on a bench by the door under the shade of two big walnut trees. At the sight of Charlotte he seemed to come to life, forgot his stick, and made to get up and walk toward her. She ran to him and made him sit down again, took a seat beside him, passed on a great many messages from her father, and then scooped up his youngest, an ugly, grubby little thing, the darling of his old age, and kissed it. I wish you could have seen how she gave herself to that old man — raising her voice for his deafness, telling him of strong young people carried off without warning, praising the waters at Carlsbad, approving his plan to spend the coming summer there, telling him he looked better and stronger than the last time she saw him. I, meanwhile, paid my respects to his wife. The old man grew quite cheerful, and since I couldn't help admiring the fine walnut trees that shaded us so pleasantly, he began, with a little effort, to tell us their story. "The older one," he said, "we don't know who planted — some say this parson, some that. But the younger one behind us is exactly my wife's age: fifty next October. Her father planted it the morning of the day she was born that evening. He was my predecessor here, and there's no telling how he loved that tree — and it's just as dear to me. My wife was sitting under it on a beam, knitting, when I first walked into this yard, a poor student, twenty-seven years ago." Charlotte asked after his daughter, and he said she'd gone out to the meadows with Herr Schmidt to see the haymakers, and then went on with his story: how his predecessor had taken a liking to him, and the daughter too, and how he'd become first the man's curate and then his successor. He'd barely finished when the daughter came back through the garden with this Herr Schmidt. She gave Charlotte a warm welcome, and I'll admit I rather liked her — a lively, good-natured brunette, the sort who could keep you amused for a while in the country. Her suitor (for that's plainly what Schmidt was) was a polite but reserved fellow who wouldn't join our talk however hard Charlotte tried to draw him out. What annoyed me most was seeing in his face that it was temper, not dullness, that kept him silent. This came out clearly later: when we set off on a walk and Frederica fell in beside Charlotte, and so beside me, the man's face, naturally rather dark, grew so black and sullen that Charlotte had to pluck my sleeve and warn me I was paying Frederica too much attention. Now nothing in the world vexes me more than to see people torment one another — above all when the young, in the very flower of life, in the very season for joy, throw away their few bright days on quarrels, and only see what they've done when it's too late to mend it. It weighed on me, and that evening, back at the vicarage and sitting round the table over our bread and milk, when the talk turned to the joys and sorrows of the world, I couldn't help laying into ill-humor. "We're forever complaining," I said, "that good days are so few and bad days so many — and usually with little reason. If our hearts were always open to take in the good that heaven sends us, we'd have the strength to bear the bad when it comes." "But we can't command our moods," said the vicar's wife. "So much depends on the body; when you're out of sorts physically, nothing feels right." "Granted," I said, "but then let's treat it as an illness and ask whether there isn't a cure."

The clergyman Charlotte and Werther visit; Frederica’s father.

"The older one," he said, "we don't know who planted — some say this parson, some that. But the younger one behind us is exactly my wife's age: fifty next October. Her father planted it the morning of the day she was born that evening. He was my predecessor here, and there's no telling how he loved that tree — and it's just as dear to me. My wife was sitting under it on a beam, knitting, when I first walked into this yard, a poor student, twenty-seven years ago." Charlotte asked after his daughter, and he said she'd gone out to the meadows with Herr Schmidt to see the haymakers, and then went on with his story: how his predecessor had taken a liking to him, and the daughter too, and how he'd become first the man's curate and then his successor. He'd barely finished when the daughter came back through the garden with this Herr Schmidt. She gave Charlotte a warm welcome, and I'll admit I rather liked her — a lively, good-natured brunette, the sort who could keep you amused for a while in the country. Her suitor (for that's plainly what Schmidt was) was a polite but reserved fellow who wouldn't join our talk however hard Charlotte tried to draw him out. What annoyed me most was seeing in his face that it was temper, not dullness, that kept him silent. This came out clearly later: when we set off on a walk and Frederica fell in beside Charlotte, and so beside me, the man's face, naturally rather dark, grew so black and sullen that Charlotte had to pluck my sleeve and warn me I was paying Frederica too much attention. Now nothing in the world vexes me more than to see people torment one another — above all when the young, in the very flower of life, in the very season for joy, throw away their few bright days on quarrels, and only see what they've done when it's too late to mend it. It weighed on me, and that evening, back at the vicarage and sitting round the table over our bread and milk, when the talk turned to the joys and sorrows of the world, I couldn't help laying into ill-humor. "We're forever complaining," I said, "that good days are so few and bad days so many — and usually with little reason. If our hearts were always open to take in the good that heaven sends us, we'd have the strength to bear the bad when it comes." "But we can't command our moods," said the vicar's wife. "So much depends on the body; when you're out of sorts physically, nothing feels right." "Granted," I said, "but then let's treat it as an illness and ask whether there isn't a cure."

The old vicar’s daughter, whose companion is Herr Schmidt.

## modern-en · madame-m · 20:0

Madame M— is very ill. I pray she'll recover, because Charlotte suffers along with her. I see Charlotte now and then at my friend's house, and today she told me the strangest thing. Old M— is a grasping, tight-fisted fellow who has long pinched and plagued the poor woman, though she's borne it patiently. A few days ago, when the doctor told us there was no hope for her, she sent for her husband — Charlotte was in the room — and said to him: "I have something to confess that could cause trouble and confusion after I'm gone. I've kept your house as thriftily and carefully as I could all these years — but you'll have to forgive me for cheating you for thirty of them. When we were first married you set aside a small sum for the kitchen and the other household needs. As our household grew and our means with it, I could never get you to raise the weekly housekeeping to match; you know that even when our needs were greatest you insisted I run everything on seven florins a week. I took the money without a word and made up the difference each week out of the cash box, since no one would suspect the mistress of robbing the till. I've wasted nothing, and I could have gone to meet my Maker without saying any of this — except that whoever takes over the house after me won't know how to manage, and you'll keep insisting that your first wife got by on the same sum."

The ailing woman Charlotte visits, married to the miserly old M—.

## modern-en · ambassador · 25:0

I can't agree to your idea that I should go with the ambassador to ——. I have no love for taking orders, and besides, we all know the man is an unpleasant brute to deal with. My mother would like to see me employed, you say — that made me laugh. Am I not employed now? And what's the real difference whether I'm counting peas or lentils? Everything in the world comes down in the end to a trifle, and a man who wears himself out for the sake of others, chasing money or honors or whatever else, without a passion or a need of his own to drive him, is a fool.

The diplomat with whom Werther is offered employment.

## modern-en · minister · 36:0

It's a calamity, Wilhelm: all my active powers have wound down to a restless idleness; I can't be doing nothing, and yet I can't get anything done either. I have no power of imagination, no feeling for nature, and books turn my stomach. Once we fail ourselves, everything fails us. I swear there are times I wish I were a day-laborer, just to have, when I wake in the morning, some prospect for the day ahead, some drive, some hope. Often I envy Albert, whom I see buried up to his ears in papers, and imagine I'd be well off in his shoes! More than once already it's come over me to write to you and to the minister, to apply for that post at the embassy which, you assure me, wouldn't be refused me. I half believe it myself. The minister has long been fond of me and has pressed me for a good while to take up some work; and for an hour at a time I'm set on it. Then, when I think it over again, the fable of the horse comes back to me — the horse that, impatient of its freedom, let itself be saddled and bridled and was ridden to death. I don't know what to do. And isn't this very longing in me for a change of state an inward, fretful impatience that would dog me wherever I went?

The government official who takes an interest in Werther’s career.

## modern-en · count-c · 41:4

I'm beginning, all things considered, to settle in here tolerably well. The best of it is that there's enough to do; and the crowd of people, all the new faces, make a colorful play before my soul. I've made the acquaintance of Count C—, a man I respect more with every day — a wide, clear mind that, just because it sees so much, is not for that reason cold, but radiates so much friendship and love. He took an interest in me when I had some business to settle with him and noticed, at the first words, that we understood each other, that he could talk with me as he can't with everyone. And I can't praise enough his open way with me. There's no joy in the world so warm, so deep, as the sight of a great soul opening itself to you.

The sympathetic nobleman Werther befriends while working at the embassy.

## modern-en · miss-b · 42:7

Lately, out walking, I made the acquaintance of a Miss B—, a charming creature who has kept much of her naturalness in the midst of all this stiff life. We took to each other in conversation, and when I left I asked leave to call on her. She granted it so frankly that I could hardly wait for the proper hour to go. She isn't from here, and lives with an aunt. The old woman's face I didn't like. I paid her a great deal of attention, addressed most of my talk to her, and in less than half an hour had pretty well guessed what the young lady afterward admitted to me herself: that her dear aunt, lacking everything in her old age — no decent fortune, no wit, and no support but the line of her ancestors, no shelter but the rank she's barricaded herself behind, and no pleasure but looking down from her upper floor over the heads of the townsfolk. She's said to have been beautiful in her youth, and to have frittered it away tormenting many a poor young fellow with her moods, and in her riper years bowed under the rule of an old officer who, for that price and a tolerable income, served out her brazen years with her and then died. Now in her iron years she's alone, and no one would so much as look at her, if her niece weren't so lovely.

The young noblewoman Werther befriends during his time at the embassy.

## modern-en · colonel · 48:1

Count O— likes me, singles me out — you know that, I've told you a hundred times. Yesterday I dined with him, on the very day his grand company of lords and ladies gathers at his house in the evening, which I never gave a thought to, and it never struck me that we underlings don't belong there. Good. I dine with the count, and after dinner we walk up and down the great hall; I talk with him, with Colonel B— who joins us, and so the hour for the party comes on. God knows, I'm thinking of nothing. In sweeps the more-than-gracious Lady von S— with her lord and her well-hatched little gosling of a daughter, with her flat chest and trim little waist; in passing they make their high-born eyes and nostrils at me in the old approved style. And since I heartily detest the whole tribe, I was just about to take my leave, only waiting for the count to be free of their wretched chatter, when my Miss B— came in. As my heart always lifts a little at the sight of her, I stayed on, stood behind her chair, and only after a while noticed that she was talking to me with less than her usual openness, a little flustered. It struck me. Is she like all the rest? I thought, and was stung, and meant to go — and yet stayed, because I wanted to excuse her, and didn't believe it, and still hoped for a kind word from her, and — whatever you like. Meanwhile the room filled up. Baron F— in a whole wardrobe inherited from the coronation of Francis I; the court councillor R—, with his deaf wife; the shabbily turned-out J—, patching the gaps of his old-fashioned coat with new bits. They came in droves, and I talked with a few of my acquaintances, who were all very curt. I was thinking — and only had eyes for my Miss B—. I didn't notice the women whispering at the far end of the room, that it spread to the men, that Frau von S— spoke to the count (Miss B— told me all this afterward), until at last the count came up to me and drew me over to a window. "You know our odd ways," he said. "The company, I notice, is displeased to see you here. I wouldn't for the world —" "Your Excellency," I broke in, "I beg your pardon a thousand times; I should have thought of it sooner, and I know you'll forgive me this inconsistency. I meant to take my leave a while ago; an evil genius held me back," I added with a smile, and bowed. The count pressed my hand with a feeling that said everything. I slipped quietly out of the grand company, got into a carriage, and drove to M—, to watch the sunset from the hill there and read in my Homer the glorious canto where Ulysses is entertained by the noble swineherd. That was good.

The officer talking with Werther and the count before the aristocratic gathering.

I slipped quietly out of the grand company, got into a carriage, and drove to M—, to watch the sunset from the hill there and read in my Homer the glorious canto where Ulysses is entertained by the noble swineherd. ## modern-en · lady-s-daughter · 48:1

Count O— likes me, singles me out — you know that, I've told you a hundred times. Yesterday I dined with him, on the very day his grand company of lords and ladies gathers at his house in the evening, which I never gave a thought to, and it never struck me that we underlings don't belong there. Good. I dine with the count, and after dinner we walk up and down the great hall; I talk with him, with Colonel B— who joins us, and so the hour for the party comes on. God knows, I'm thinking of nothing. In sweeps the more-than-gracious Lady von S— with her lord and her well-hatched little gosling of a daughter, with her flat chest and trim little waist; in passing they make their high-born eyes and nostrils at me in the old approved style. And since I heartily detest the whole tribe, I was just about to take my leave, only waiting for the count to be free of their wretched chatter, when my Miss B— came in. As my heart always lifts a little at the sight of her, I stayed on, stood behind her chair, and only after a while noticed that she was talking to me with less than her usual openness, a little flustered. It struck me. Is she like all the rest? I thought, and was stung, and meant to go — and yet stayed, because I wanted to excuse her, and didn't believe it, and still hoped for a kind word from her, and — whatever you like. Meanwhile the room filled up. Baron F— in a whole wardrobe inherited from the coronation of Francis I; the court councillor R—, with his deaf wife; the shabbily turned-out J—, patching the gaps of his old-fashioned coat with new bits. They came in droves, and I talked with a few of my acquaintances, who were all very curt. I was thinking — and only had eyes for my Miss B—. I didn't notice the women whispering at the far end of the room, that it spread to the men, that Frau von S— spoke to the count (Miss B— told me all this afterward), until at last the count came up to me and drew me over to a window. "You know our odd ways," he said. "The company, I notice, is displeased to see you here. I wouldn't for the world —" "Your Excellency," I broke in, "I beg your pardon a thousand times; I should have thought of it sooner, and I know you'll forgive me this inconsistency. I meant to take my leave a while ago; an evil genius held me back," I added with a smile, and bowed. The count pressed my hand with a feeling that said everything. I slipped quietly out of the grand company, got into a carriage, and drove to M—, to watch the sunset from the hill there and read in my Homer the glorious canto where Ulysses is entertained by the noble swineherd. That was good.

The young woman attending the gathering with her parents.

## modern-en · adelin · 48:6

Everything's after me. Today I met Miss B— out walking; I couldn't help speaking to her, and as soon as we were a little away from the others I told her how hurt I'd been by her manner the other day. "Oh, Werther," she said warmly, "how could you read my confusion that way, you who know my heart? What I suffered for you, from the moment I stepped into the room! I saw it all coming; a hundred times it was on the tip of my tongue to warn you. I knew the von S—s and the von T—s would sooner leave with their husbands than stay in your company; I knew the count couldn't afford to break with them — and now this fuss!" "What do you mean?" I said, hiding my fright — for everything Adelin had told me the day before ran like boiling water through my veins in that instant. "What it has already cost me!" said the sweet creature, with tears in her eyes. I was no longer master of myself, ready to throw myself at her feet. "Tell me what you mean!" I cried. The tears ran down her cheeks; I was beside myself. She wiped them away without trying to hide them. "You know my aunt," she began. "She was there and saw it all — with what eyes, Werther! Last night, and this morning, I've had to hear a sermon about my acquaintance with you, sit there and listen to you belittled and run down, and could not — dared not — defend you fully."

The acquaintance who tells Werther about the reaction to his presence at the gathering.
