## modern-en · prince · 49:0

I've sent in my resignation to the court, and I hope it will be accepted; forgive me for not asking your leave first. I have to get away, and I know all you'll say to talk me into staying, so — sweeten it for my mother. I can't help myself, so she'll have to put up with my not being able to help her either. It will hurt her, of course, to see the fine career her son had begun — privy councillor, ambassador, and who knows what — pulled up short, and the little horse stabled again. Make of it what you will, reckon up all the ways and means by which I might have stayed: enough, I'm going. But so you'll know where I'm bound — there's a Prince of —— here who has taken a great liking to my company; when he heard of my plans he asked me to come with him to his estates and spend the lovely spring there. I'm to be left entirely to myself, he promises, and since the two of us understand each other up to a point, I'll chance it and go with him.

The nobleman who invites Werther to stay at his country house.

## modern-en · crown-prince · 50:0

Thank you for both your letters. I didn't answer because I left this sheet lying until my discharge should come through from the court; I was afraid my mother might turn to the minister and make my plan harder. But now it's done, my discharge has come. I won't tell you how reluctantly they granted it, nor what the minister wrote — you'd only start your lamenting again. The crown prince has sent me a parting gift of twenty-five ducats, with a word that moved me to tears; so I won't need the money from my mother that I asked for lately.

The royal patron who sends Werther money when he leaves his post.

## modern-en · new-vicar · 65:0

It's enough to drive a man mad, Wilhelm, that there should be people without sense or feeling for the few things on earth that still have any worth. You remember the walnut trees I sat under with Charlotte at the good vicar's at St—, those glorious trees that, God knows, always filled my soul with the greatest delight! How homely they made the parsonage yard, how cool, and how splendid the branches were; and the memory going back to the good clergymen who planted them so many years ago. The schoolmaster often gave us one of their names, that he'd heard from his grandfather; a fine man he's said to have been, and his memory was always sacred to me under those trees. I tell you, the schoolmaster had tears in his eyes yesterday when we spoke of how they'd been cut down. Cut down! I could go mad, I could kill the dog that struck the first blow. I, who could grieve myself to death if a couple of such trees stood in my yard and one died of old age — I have to stand by and watch this. But there's one thing, dear friend — there is such a thing as human feeling! The whole village grumbles, and I hope the vicar's wife will feel in her butter and eggs and the rest of it what a wound she's dealt the place. For it was she — the new incumbent's wife (our old one has died too) — a lean, sickly thing with good reason to take no part in the world, since no one takes any part in her. A fool who fancies herself learned, dabbles in the canon, works away busily at the newfangled moral-critical reform of Christianity, and shrugs her shoulders at Lavater's raptures; her health quite broken, so that she has no joy in God's earth. Only such a creature could cut down my walnut trees. You see, I can't get over it! Just imagine: the falling leaves make her yard wet and moldy; the trees rob her of daylight; and when the nuts are ripe the boys throw stones at them, and it jars her nerves, disturbs her deep musings as she weighs Kennicott against Semler and Michaelis. When I saw how put out the people were, the old ones especially, I said, "Why did you let it happen?" "When the bailiff wants a thing, here in the country," they said, "what's to be done?" But one thing pleased me. The bailiff and the vicar, who meant at least to make something off his wife's whims — the trees were to be theirs — wanted to split the profit; but the treasury heard of it, said, "This way," and bought the trees, for it had an old claim to that bit of ground. Down they lie, sold. Oh, if I were the prince! I'd know what to do with the vicar's wife, the bailiff, and the treasury — prince! Ah, if I were prince, what would I care about the trees in my own land!

The clergyman who succeeds Frederica’s father.

## modern-en · ill-n · 69:0

Yes, it grows certain to me, dear friend, certain and ever more certain, that the being of a single creature counts for little, very little. A friend of Charlotte's came to see her, and I went into the next room to find a book, and couldn't read, and then took up a pen to write. I heard them talking softly; they were telling each other trifles, the town news: this one's getting married, that one is sick, very sick — "a dry cough, the bones standing out in her face, and fainting fits; I wouldn't give a penny for her life," said the one. "And N— is in a bad way too," said Charlotte. "He's all swollen up," said the other. And my lively imagination set me down at the bedside of these poor people; I saw them turn from life with all their reluctance, saw them — and Wilhelm, my little ladies talked of it exactly as one talks of a stranger's dying. And when I look around me and look around this room, and see Charlotte's clothes lying about, and Albert's papers, and the furniture I'm so at home with, even this inkwell, I think: see what you are to this house! All in all. Your friends honor you, you often make their joy, and it seems to your heart it couldn't beat without them — and yet — if you went now, if you left this circle — would they feel, and how long would they feel, the gap your loss tears in their fate? How long? Oh, man is so fleeting that even there, where he has the one sure sense of his own being, where he makes the one true mark — even in the memory, in the soul of those he loves — there too he must be blotted out, must vanish, and that so soon!

The man whose illness Charlotte mentions in conversation.

## modern-en · henry · 80:1

I went down by the water at noon; I had no appetite. Everything was bleak; a cold, wet west wind blew off the mountains, and gray rain-clouds drove into the valley. From a distance I saw a man in a shabby green coat scrambling among the rocks, who seemed to be looking for plants. As I came nearer and he turned at the sound, I saw a very interesting face, whose chief mark was a quiet sadness, but otherwise nothing but an honest, good nature; his black hair was pinned up in two rolls, the rest braided in a thick plait down his back. As his dress seemed to mark him a man of humble station, I thought he wouldn't take it amiss if I noticed what he was at, and so I asked what he was looking for. "I'm looking," he answered with a deep sigh, "for flowers — and find none." "But it isn't the season for them," I said, smiling. "There are so many flowers," he said, coming down to me. "In my garden there are roses and two kinds of honeysuckle, one my father gave me; they grow like weeds; I've been looking for them two days now and can't find them. Out here there are always flowers too, yellow and blue and red, and the centaury has a pretty little blossom. I can't find a one of them." I noticed something uncanny, and so asked, in a roundabout way, what he wanted the flowers for. A strange, twitching smile drew over his face. "If you won't give me away," he said, pressing a finger to his lips, "I promised my sweetheart a posy." "That's good of you," I said. "Oh," he said, "she has lots of other things; she's rich." "And still she likes your posy," I said. "Oh, she has jewels, and a crown!" "What's her name, then?" "If only the States-General would pay me," he answered, "I'd be a different man! Yes, there was once a time when I was so well off! Now it's all over with me. Now I'm —" A wet look up to heaven said all. "So you were happy?" I said. "Ah, I wish I were so again!" he said. "Then I felt so well, so glad, so light, like a fish in water!" "Heinrich!" cried an old woman coming up the path. "Heinrich, where are you hiding? We've looked all over for you; come and eat." "Is that your son?" I asked, going to her. "Yes, my poor son," she answered. "God has laid a heavy cross on me." "How long has he been like this?" I asked. "This quiet," she said, "he's only been half a year. Thank God he's got this far; before that he raged a whole year, lay chained in the madhouse. Now he does no one any harm, only he's always meddling with kings and emperors. He was such a good, quiet lad, who helped feed me, wrote a fine hand, and then all at once he turned melancholy, fell into a violent fever, from there into madness, and now he's as you see him. If I were to tell you, sir —" I cut short her flood and asked, "And what was that time he praises so, when he was so happy, so well off?" "The poor fool," she cried with a pitying smile, "he means the time he was out of his mind; he never stops praising it — the time he was in the madhouse and knew nothing of himself." It struck me like a thunderclap; I pressed a coin into her hand and hurried off.

The distressed man Werther encounters looking for flowers.

## modern-en · servant · 23:0

Wilhelm, what is the world to our hearts without love? A magic lantern without its lamp. The moment you set the little flame inside it, the brightest pictures shine out on your white wall! And even if they're nothing but passing phantoms, still it makes us happy when we stand before them like children, carried away by the wonderful sight. Today I couldn't get to Charlotte — visitors I couldn't shake off held me back. What was I to do? I sent my servant over, just to have someone near me today who'd been near her. How impatiently I waited for him, how overjoyed I was to see him back! I'd have caught him up and kissed him, if I hadn't been ashamed.

The servant who attends to Werther’s household and errands.

## modern-en · charlotte-servant · 84:57

She thought of having the maid sit with her work in the next room; then changed her mind again. Werther paced the room; she went to the piano and began a minuet, but it wouldn't come right. She pulled herself together and sat down calmly beside Werther, who had taken his usual place on the sofa.

The servant present at Charlotte’s house during Werther’s visit.

## modern-en · albert-host · 32:0

Albert is certainly the best fellow alive. I had a strange scene with him yesterday. I'd gone to take my leave, since the whim took me to ride up into these hills, from which I'm writing to you now. As I was pacing his room my eye fell on his pistols. "Lend me the pistols," I said, "for the journey." "Gladly," he said, "if you'll take the trouble to load them; they only hang there for show." I took one down, and he went on, "Ever since my caution played me such a nasty trick, I won't have anything more to do with the things." I was curious to hear the story. "I was staying about three months," he said, "at a friend's place in the country. I had a pair of unloaded pocket-pistols, and slept easy. One rainy afternoon, sitting about with nothing to do, it came into my head — I don't know how — that we might be attacked, that we might need the pistols, that — well, you know how it goes when one has nothing better to do. I handed them to the servant to clean and load. He was fooling with the maid, trying to scare her, and somehow — God knows how — the gun went off, the ramrod still down the barrel, and drove it into the ball of her right thumb and smashed it. So I had her wailing to put up with and the surgeon's bill to pay into the bargain, and ever since I've kept my weapons unloaded. My dear fellow, what good is caution? You can't learn every danger in advance. To be sure —" Now, you must know, I can get along with a man right up to his "to be sure"; for surely it goes without saying that every general rule has its exceptions. But the man is so terribly careful that the moment he thinks he's said something a bit too rash, too sweeping, only half true, he won't stop qualifying and trimming and whittling away until in the end there's nothing left of it at all. And on this occasion he got so deep into the text that I stopped listening to him altogether and fell to brooding, and with one sudden motion I pressed the muzzle of the pistol to my forehead, above the right eye. "Good God," cried Albert, pulling the pistol down, "what is this?" "It isn't loaded," I said. "Even so — what do you mean by it?" he snapped. "I can't imagine how a man could be foolish enough to shoot himself; the very thought disgusts me."

The friend whose country house appears in Albert’s pistol anecdote.

## modern-en · surgeon · 84:114

In the morning at six the servant came in with a light. He found his master on the floor, the pistol, and blood. He called out, took hold of him; no answer, only a rattle still in his throat. ## modern-en · homer · 5:0

You ask whether you should send me books. Dear friend, for the love of God, spare me that yoke! I don't want to be guided, stirred up, inflamed any more — my heart seethes quite enough on its own. What I need is something to lull me, and I find it perfectly in my Homer. How often I try to quiet the fever in my blood; you have never seen anything so changeable, so unsteady, as this heart of mine. But I hardly need confess that to you, who have so often had to watch me lurch from grief to wild joy, from sweet melancholy to violent passion. I treat my poor heart like a sick child and let it have whatever it wants. Don't repeat this — there are people who would hold it against me.

The ancient Greek poet whose works Werther reads.

## modern-en · batteux · 7:3

A few days ago I met a young man named V—, a frank, open fellow with a very pleasant face. He has just come from the university; he doesn't think himself especially wise, but he does believe he knows more than other people. He has worked hard, as I can tell in all sorts of ways — in short, he is well informed. When he heard that I draw a good deal and that I know Greek (two rare things in this part of the country), he sought me out and unloaded his whole stock of learning, from Batteux to Wood, from De Piles to Winckelmann; he assured me he had read all the way through the first part of Sulzer's theory and owned a manuscript of Heyne's work on the study of antiquity. I let it all pass.

The French writer on literature and the arts named by V—.

## modern-en · klopstock · 12:24

I saw more than one person pucker up and stretch in pleasant anticipation of a forfeit. "Let's play counting," said Charlotte. "Now pay attention. I'll go round the circle from right to left, and each of you in turn calls out his number — count quickly; whoever hesitates or slips gets a box on the ear, and so on up to a thousand." It was a joy to watch. She went round the ring with her arm raised. "One," said the first; "two," the next; "three," the third; and so on, while she went faster and faster. Then someone slipped — smack, a box on the ear — and amid the laughter the next one slipped too, and so on, faster and faster. I caught two boxes myself, and was delighted to fancy they were harder than the rest. A general burst of laughter and uproar broke the game off well before we reached a thousand. The party drifted apart into little knots; the storm had passed, and I followed Charlotte back into the ballroom. On the way she said, "The forfeits made them forget all about the storm." I couldn't say a word. "I was as frightened as any of them," she went on, "but by acting brave to keep the others' courage up I forgot my own fear." We went to the window. Thunder was still rolling in the distance; a soft rain was falling over the land, and the most delicious fragrance rose to us on the warm air. She stood there leaning on her elbow, her gaze searching the countryside; she looked up at the sky and then at me, her eyes full of tears; she laid her hand on mine and said, "Klopstock!" At once I knew the magnificent ode in her mind, and I sank under the flood of feeling that the one word loosed in me. It was more than I could bear. I bent over her hand and kissed it through tears of bliss, and looked again into her eyes. Noble poet! If only you could have seen your worship in that look — and may I never again hear your name, so often profaned, on lesser lips!

The German poet whose ode Charlotte and Werther recall during the storm.

## modern-en · ernesti · 37:0

If my troubles would let themselves be cured, these people here would surely do it. Today is my birthday, and first thing in the morning a little parcel came from Albert. As I opened it, the first thing my eye lit on was one of the pink bows Charlotte wore the first time I saw her, and which I'd begged from her several times since. With it came two little duodecimo volumes — Wetstein's small Homer, an edition I'd often wanted, so as not to lug the big Ernesti one about on my walks. There — that's how they get ahead of my wishes, how they find out all the little favors of friendship that are worth a thousand times more than the dazzling gifts by which a giver humbles us. I kiss the bow a thousand times, and with every breath I drink in the memory of the blessings those few happy, never-to-return days poured over me. Wilhelm, that's how it is, and I don't complain: the blossoms of life are only show. How many fall and leave no trace, how few set fruit, and how few of those ripen! And yet there are still enough; and yet — oh my brother — can we let the ripened fruit go neglected, despise it, leave it to rot unenjoyed? Farewell. It's a glorious summer; I often sit up in the fruit trees in Charlotte's orchard with the long pole and pick the pears from the highest branches. She stands below and takes them as I hand them down.

The classical scholar associated with Werther’s larger edition of Homer.

## modern-en · francis · 48:1

Count O— likes me, singles me out — you know that, I've told you a hundred times. Yesterday I dined with him, on the very day his grand company of lords and ladies gathers at his house in the evening, which I never gave a thought to, and it never struck me that we underlings don't belong there. Good. I dine with the count, and after dinner we walk up and down the great hall; I talk with him, with Colonel B— who joins us, and so the hour for the party comes on. God knows, I'm thinking of nothing. In sweeps the more-than-gracious Lady von S— with her lord and her well-hatched little gosling of a daughter, with her flat chest and trim little waist; in passing they make their high-born eyes and nostrils at me in the old approved style. And since I heartily detest the whole tribe, I was just about to take my leave, only waiting for the count to be free of their wretched chatter, when my Miss B— came in. As my heart always lifts a little at the sight of her, I stayed on, stood behind her chair, and only after a while noticed that she was talking to me with less than her usual openness, a little flustered. It struck me. Is she like all the rest? I thought, and was stung, and meant to go — and yet stayed, because I wanted to excuse her, and didn't believe it, and still hoped for a kind word from her, and — whatever you like. Meanwhile the room filled up. Baron F— in a whole wardrobe inherited from the coronation of Francis I; the court councillor R—, with his deaf wife; the shabbily turned-out J—, patching the gaps of his old-fashioned coat with new bits. They came in droves, and I talked with a few of my acquaintances, who were all very curt. I was thinking — and only had eyes for my Miss B—. I didn't notice the women whispering at the far end of the room, that it spread to the men, that Frau von S— spoke to the count (Miss B— told me all this afterward), until at last the count came up to me and drew me over to a window. "You know our odd ways," he said. "The company, I notice, is displeased to see you here. I wouldn't for the world —" "Your Excellency," I broke in, "I beg your pardon a thousand times; I should have thought of it sooner, and I know you'll forgive me this inconsistency. I meant to take my leave a while ago; an evil genius held me back," I added with a smile, and bowed. The count pressed my hand with a feeling that said everything. I slipped quietly out of the grand company, got into a carriage, and drove to M—, to watch the sunset from the hill there and read in my Homer the glorious canto where Ulysses is entertained by the noble swineherd. That was good.

The Holy Roman emperor whose coronation dates Baron F—’s old-fashioned suit.

## modern-en · lavater · 65:0

It's enough to drive a man mad, Wilhelm, that there should be people without sense or feeling for the few things on earth that still have any worth. You remember the walnut trees I sat under with Charlotte at the good vicar's at St—, those glorious trees that, God knows, always filled my soul with the greatest delight! How homely they made the parsonage yard, how cool, and how splendid the branches were; and the memory going back to the good clergymen who planted them so many years ago. The schoolmaster often gave us one of their names, that he'd heard from his grandfather; a fine man he's said to have been, and his memory was always sacred to me under those trees. I tell you, the schoolmaster had tears in his eyes yesterday when we spoke of how they'd been cut down. Cut down! I could go mad, I could kill the dog that struck the first blow. I, who could grieve myself to death if a couple of such trees stood in my yard and one died of old age — I have to stand by and watch this. But there's one thing, dear friend — there is such a thing as human feeling! The whole village grumbles, and I hope the vicar's wife will feel in her butter and eggs and the rest of it what a wound she's dealt the place. For it was she — the new incumbent's wife (our old one has died too) — a lean, sickly thing with good reason to take no part in the world, since no one takes any part in her. A fool who fancies herself learned, dabbles in the canon, works away busily at the newfangled moral-critical reform of Christianity, and shrugs her shoulders at Lavater's raptures; her health quite broken, so that she has no joy in God's earth. Only such a creature could cut down my walnut trees. You see, I can't get over it! Just imagine: the falling leaves make her yard wet and moldy; the trees rob her of daylight; and when the nuts are ripe the boys throw stones at them, and it jars her nerves, disturbs her deep musings as she weighs Kennicott against Semler and Michaelis. When I saw how put out the people were, the old ones especially, I said, "Why did you let it happen?" "When the bailiff wants a thing, here in the country," they said, "what's to be done?" But one thing pleased me. The bailiff and the vicar, who meant at least to make something off his wife's whims — the trees were to be theirs — wanted to split the profit; but the treasury heard of it, said, "This way," and bought the trees, for it had an old claim to that bit of ground. Down they lie, sold. Oh, if I were the prince! I'd know what to do with the vicar's wife, the bailiff, and the treasury — prince! Ah, if I were prince, what would I care about the trees in my own land!

The Swiss religious writer mentioned in the account of the new vicar’s wife.

## modern-en · melusina · 4:0

I can't tell whether deceiving spirits hover over this place, or whether it is the warm, heavenly imagination in my own heart that turns everything around me into paradise. Just in front of the house there is a spring — a spring that holds me under a spell, like Melusina and her sisters. You go down a gentle slope and come to an archway, and some twenty steps further down the clearest crystal water gushes from the marble rock. The low wall that encloses it at the top, the tall trees that ring the spot, the coolness of the place itself — it all makes an impression at once pleasant and solemn. Not a day goes by that I don't spend an hour there. The young girls come out from town to fetch water — innocent, necessary work that once even kings' daughters did. When I sit and rest there, the old patriarchal life comes alive around me: I can see them, our forefathers, striking up friendships and arranging matches at the well, and I feel how kindly spirits watched over the springs and streams. Anyone who can't share these feelings has never really known the relief of resting by cool water after a hard summer's day.

The water spirit of European legend, invoked beside the fountain.

## modern-en · penelope · 14:5

When I walk out to Walheim at sunrise and there in the garden pick my own peas for dinner, and sit down to shell them while I read my Homer between handfuls; when I choose a pot in the little kitchen, cut myself a pat of butter, set my peas on the fire, cover them, and sit by to give them a stir now and then — I can feel so vividly how Penelope's overbearing suitors slaughtered and carved and roasted their own oxen and swine. Nothing fills me with so quiet and true a sense of happiness as these touches of patriarchal life, which, thank heaven, I can weave into my own without affectation. How glad I am that my heart can feel the simple, harmless joy of a man who brings to his table a cabbage he grew himself, and who in that one moment relives not just the cabbage but all the good days — the fine morning he planted it, the soft evenings he watered it, the pleasure he took in watching it grow — all of it together again.

Odysseus’s wife in Homer’s Odyssey.

## modern-en · fictitious-leonora · 12:12

## modern-en · jesus · 15:1

Yes, dear Wilhelm, nothing on this earth touches my heart like children. When I watch them, and see in the little creatures the seeds of every virtue, every strength they'll one day so badly need; when I make out in the stubborn child the firmness and steadiness of a fine character to come, and in the unruly one the good humor and lightness that will carry him safely over life's dangers — and all of it still whole, still unspoiled — then I say over to myself again and again the golden words of the Teacher of mankind: "Unless you become as one of these!" And yet, my friend, these children, who are our equals, whom we ought to take as our models, we treat as our subjects. They're to have no will of their own. And have we none, then? Where does our great privilege come from? Because we're older and wiser? Good God in heaven, you see only big children and little children, and nothing else; and your Son said long ago which give you the greater joy. But they believe in him and don't listen to him — that too is an old story — and they shape their children after themselves, and so on.

The Christian teacher whose words Werther recalls.

## modern-en · levite · 32:5

"Oh, you reasonable people!" I cried, smiling. "Passion! Drunkenness! Madness! There you stand, so cool, so unconcerned, you moral men — scolding the drunkard, loathing the madman, passing by on the other side like the Levite, and thanking God like the Pharisee that he hasn't made you as one of them. I've been drunk more than once, my passions have never been far from madness, and I'm not ashamed of either; for I've learned, in my own way, that all extraordinary men who ever did something great, something that seemed impossible, have always been cried down as drunk or mad. And in ordinary life too it's unbearable to hear it said of nearly anyone who does a halfway free, noble, unexpected thing: the man is drunk, he's a fool! Shame on you sober ones, shame on you sages!"

The passerby from the Good Samaritan parable, used as a comparison.

## modern-en · samaritan · 84:109

## modern-en · god · 2:0

How glad I am to be away! Dear friend, what a strange thing the human heart is! To leave you — you whom I have been inseparable from, whom I love so dearly — and still to feel happy! I know you will forgive me. Weren't my other attachments practically appointed by fate to torment a mind like mine? Poor Leonora! And yet I was not to blame. Was it my fault that, while her sister's particular charms gave me such pleasant company, a passion took root in her own fragile heart? And yet — am I entirely innocent? Didn't I encourage her feelings? Didn't I delight in those wholly genuine expressions of nature that, though hardly comic in themselves, so often made us laugh? Didn't I — oh, what is a man, that he dares accuse himself like this? I promise you, dear friend, I will do better; I will no longer chew over every small misfortune that fate deals out, the way I always have; I will enjoy the present, and let the past stay past. You are right, of course, my best of friends — there would be far less suffering among us if people didn't work their imaginations so hard recalling old griefs, instead of bearing the present with some composure, though God knows why we are made this way. Please tell my mother I will handle her business as well as I can and send her word of it as soon as possible. I have seen my aunt, and she is nothing like the disagreeable woman our relatives make her out to be. She is lively and warm, with the kindest heart. I explained my mother's grievance about the part of her share that has been kept back. My aunt told me her own reasons and motives, and the terms on which she is willing to hand over the whole of it — and to do more than we asked. I can't write any more about it just now; only assure my mother that it will all come right. And once again, dear friend, this small affair has shown me that misunderstanding and neglect cause more trouble in the world than even malice and cruelty do. At least the last two are rarer.

The divine being Werther and others address in prayer and reflection.

## modern-en · fingal · 67:0

Ossian has driven Homer out of my heart. What a world the glorious bard leads me into! To roam over the heath, the storm-wind raging around, leading the spirits of the fathers in the smoking mist by the dim light of the moon; to hear from the hills, half-drowned in the roar of the mountain torrent, the fading groans of the spirits in their caves, and the laments of the girl grieving herself to death by the four moss-grown, grassy stones of the hero, her beloved, fallen. Then I find him, the gray wandering bard, seeking on the wide heath the footprints of his fathers, and finding, alas, their gravestones, and looking up with a wail to the dear evening star that hides in the rolling sea, and the ages of the past wake to life in the hero's soul, when the kindly beam still lit the brave through their dangers, and the moon shone down on their garlanded, homecoming ship. When I read the deep grief on his brow, see the last forsaken, glorious one stagger weary toward the grave, drinking ever new, painful-glowing joys from the powerless presence of his dead, and looking down at the cold earth, at the tall waving grass, and crying: "The wanderer will come, will come, who knew me in my beauty, and ask, Where is the singer, Fingal's noble son? His step passes over my grave, and he asks for me in vain over the earth." Then, O friend, like a noble squire I'd draw the sword and free my prince at one stroke from the quivering torment of a slow-dying life — and send my own soul after the freed half-god.

The heroic king in the Ossian poems.

## modern-en · colma-father · 84:63

Come out, moon, from behind your clouds! Show yourselves, stars of the night! Let some light lead me to the place where my love rests from the labor of the hunt — his slack bow beside him, his dogs panting around him. But here I must sit alone on the rock by the mossy stream. The stream and the wind roar; I can't hear my love's voice! Why does my Salgar wait? Has he forgotten his word? Here is the rock, and the tree, and the rushing stream! You promised to be here at nightfall. Ah, where has my Salgar gone? With you I'd flee my father, with you my proud brother. Our houses have long been enemies — but we are not enemies, O Salgar!

The father Colma would leave to be with Salgar.

## modern-en · torman · 84:67

That was your song, Minona, soft-blushing daughter of Torman. Our tears fell for Colma, and our souls grew dark. Then Ullin came with the harp and gave us Alpin's song. Alpin's voice was kind, Ryno's soul a beam of fire; but already they rested in the narrow house, and their voice had died away in Selma. Once, before the heroes fell, Ullin came back from the hunt; he heard their contest of song on the hill. Their song was soft but sad. They mourned the fall of Morar, first of men; his soul was like Fingal's soul, his sword like Oscar's. But he fell, and his father wailed, and his sister's eyes were full of tears — Minona's eyes, the sister of the great Morar. She drew back before Ullin's song, as the moon in the west draws back when she feels the storm coming and hides her lovely head in a cloud. I struck the harp with Ullin, for the song of mourning.

The father of Minona and Morar in the Ossian poems.

## modern-en · morar-mother · 84:71

Narrow is your dwelling now, dark your home! With three strides I measure your grave, O you who were once so great! Four stones, moss on their heads, are all that's left of you; a leafless tree, tall grass whistling in the wind, show the hunter's eye the grave of the mighty Morar. No mother is left to weep for you, no girl with the tears of love. Dead is she who bore you; fallen the daughter of Morglan.

The mother mentioned in the lament for Morar.

## modern-en · armin · 84:73

Loud was the grief of all, but loudest the broken sigh of Armin. It called back the death of his son, fallen in his young days. Carmor sat near the hero, the chief of the sounding Galmal. "Why does Armin sob so?" he said. "What is there here to weep for? Doesn't song ring out, and music, to melt and gladden the soul? It is like the soft mist that rises from the lake and spreads over the valley, so that the flowers fill with dew; but the sun comes back in its strength, and the mist is gone. Why so cast down, Armin, lord of sea-girt Gorma?"

The chief of Gorma and father of Daura and Arindal in the Ossian song.

## modern-en · colgar · 84:74

Cast down? Indeed I am, and not without cause for grief. Carmor, you have lost no son, you have lost no blooming daughter. Colgar the brave lives, and Annira, fairest of maidens. The branches of your house flourish, O Carmor; but Armin is the last of his line. Dark is your bed, Daura, and heavy your sleep in the grave. When will you wake again with your songs, with your sweet voice?

Carmor’s son in the Ossian song.

## modern-en · arindal · 84:75

Rise, you winds of autumn, rise! Storm over the dark heath! Roar, you streams of the woods! Howl, you tempests, in the crowns of the oaks! Stride, O moon, through the broken clouds, and let your pale face shine out now and then! Bring back to me the night of horror when all my children fell — when Arindal the strong fell, when Daura the lovely passed away. Daura, my daughter, you were beautiful, fair as the moon on the hills of Fura, white as fallen snow, sweet as the breathing air! Arindal, your bow was strong, your spear swift in the field, your look like mist on the wave, your shield a red cloud in the storm. Armar, famed in war, came and sought Daura's love; she did not long refuse, and fair were their friends' hopes.

Armin’s son and Daura’s brother.

## modern-en · erath · 84:76

But Erath, Odgal's son, raged, for his brother had fallen by Armar's hand. He came disguised as a boatman; fair was his skiff on the wave, white his hair with age, calm his grave face. "Loveliest of girls," he said, "fair daughter of Armin! Out on the sea, not far, a rock bears a tree, its red fruit shining from afar. There Armar waits for Daura; I am come to bring his love across the rolling sea." She went with him; she called for Armar — and nothing answered but the voice of the rock. "Armar! my love! my love! why do you torment me so with fear? Hear me, son of Arnart, hear! It is Daura who calls you!" Erath, the traitor, fled to land, laughing. She lifted her voice, called for her father, her brother: "Arindal! Armin! Is there none to save your Daura?"

The son of Odgal whose grievance against Armar enters the song of Daura.

## modern-en · werther-father · 51:0

I leave here tomorrow, and since my birthplace lies only six miles off the road, I want to see it again, to remember the old days and their dreams. I'll go in by the same gate my mother drove out of with me when, after my father's death, she left the dear, familiar place to shut herself up in her unbearable town. Adieu, Wilhelm; you'll hear how the journey goes.

Werther’s late father, remembered in the account of leaving his childhood home.

## modern-en · albert-father · 12:7

I mentioned the other day that I'd got to know S—, the district steward, and that he'd invited me out to see him in his retreat — his little kingdom, rather. I put it off, and might never have gone if chance hadn't shown me the treasure hidden away in that quiet spot. Some of our young people had got up a country ball, and I agreed to go. I offered to escort a pretty, pleasant, but rather ordinary girl from the neighborhood, and it was settled that I'd hire a carriage and drive out with my partner and her aunt to collect Charlotte on the way. "You'll meet a very lovely young woman," my companion told me as we drove through the woods toward the hunting lodge. "Take care you don't lose your heart," the aunt added. "Why?" I asked. "Because she's already promised to a very fine man," she said, "who's away just now settling his affairs after his father's death, and stands to come into a sizable inheritance." The news meant nothing to me. As we reached the gate the sun was just going down behind the mountaintops. The air was heavy, and the ladies fretted about a storm coming on, with banks of low black cloud massing on the horizon. I soothed them by playing the weather prophet, though I had my own misgivings that our evening might be spoiled.

Albert’s late father, whose affairs Albert has gone to settle.

## modern-en · henry-father · 80:1

I went down by the water at noon; I had no appetite. Everything was bleak; a cold, wet west wind blew off the mountains, and gray rain-clouds drove into the valley. From a distance I saw a man in a shabby green coat scrambling among the rocks, who seemed to be looking for plants. As I came nearer and he turned at the sound, I saw a very interesting face, whose chief mark was a quiet sadness, but otherwise nothing but an honest, good nature; his black hair was pinned up in two rolls, the rest braided in a thick plait down his back. As his dress seemed to mark him a man of humble station, I thought he wouldn't take it amiss if I noticed what he was at, and so I asked what he was looking for. "I'm looking," he answered with a deep sigh, "for flowers — and find none." "But it isn't the season for them," I said, smiling. "There are so many flowers," he said, coming down to me. "In my garden there are roses and two kinds of honeysuckle, one my father gave me; they grow like weeds; I've been looking for them two days now and can't find them. Out here there are always flowers too, yellow and blue and red, and the centaury has a pretty little blossom. I can't find a one of them." I noticed something uncanny, and so asked, in a roundabout way, what he wanted the flowers for. A strange, twitching smile drew over his face. "If you won't give me away," he said, pressing a finger to his lips, "I promised my sweetheart a posy." "That's good of you," I said. "Oh," he said, "she has lots of other things; she's rich." "And still she likes your posy," I said. "Oh, she has jewels, and a crown!" "What's her name, then?" "If only the States-General would pay me," he answered, "I'd be a different man! Yes, there was once a time when I was so well off! Now it's all over with me. Now I'm —" A wet look up to heaven said all. "So you were happy?" I said. "Ah, I wish I were so again!" he said. "Then I felt so well, so glad, so light, like a fish in water!" "Heinrich!" cried an old woman coming up the path. "Heinrich, where are you hiding? We've looked all over for you; come and eat." "Is that your son?" I asked, going to her. "Yes, my poor son," she answered. "God has laid a heavy cross on me." "How long has he been like this?" I asked. "This quiet," she said, "he's only been half a year. Thank God he's got this far; before that he raged a whole year, lay chained in the madhouse. Now he does no one any harm, only he's always meddling with kings and emperors. He was such a good, quiet lad, who helped feed me, wrote a fine hand, and then all at once he turned melancholy, fell into a violent fever, from there into madness, and now he's as you see him. If I were to tell you, sir —" I cut short her flood and asked, "And what was that time he praises so, when he was so happy, so well off?" "The poor fool," she cried with a pitying smile, "he means the time he was out of his mind; he never stops praising it — the time he was in the madhouse and knew nothing of himself." It struck me like a thunderclap; I pressed a coin into her hand and hurried off.

The father Henry mentions while talking about flowers.

## modern-en · well-girl · 6:2

The other day I went down to the spring and found a young servant girl who had set her pitcher on the bottom step and was looking round to see whether one of her friends was coming to help lift it onto her head. I went down and looked at her. "Shall I help you, my dear?" I said. She turned bright red. "Oh no, sir!" she said. "Don't stand on ceremony," I replied. She straightened the pad on her head, and I helped her. She thanked me and climbed the steps.

The servant girl whose pitcher Werther helps lift.

## modern-en · wetstein · 37:0

If my troubles would let themselves be cured, these people here would surely do it. Today is my birthday, and first thing in the morning a little parcel came from Albert. As I opened it, the first thing my eye lit on was one of the pink bows Charlotte wore the first time I saw her, and which I'd begged from her several times since. With it came two little duodecimo volumes — Wetstein's small Homer, an edition I'd often wanted, so as not to lug the big Ernesti one about on my walks. There — that's how they get ahead of my wishes, how they find out all the little favors of friendship that are worth a thousand times more than the dazzling gifts by which a giver humbles us. I kiss the bow a thousand times, and with every breath I drink in the memory of the blessings those few happy, never-to-return days poured over me. Wilhelm, that's how it is, and I don't complain: the blossoms of life are only show. How many fall and leave no trace, how few set fruit, and how few of those ripen! And yet there are still enough; and yet — oh my brother — can we let the ripened fruit go neglected, despise it, leave it to rot unenjoyed? Farewell. It's a glorious summer; I often sit up in the fruit trees in Charlotte's orchard with the long pole and pick the pears from the highest branches. She stands below and takes them as I hand them down.

The publishing name on the small edition of Homer given to Werther.

## modern-en · w · 18:0

What a child a man is, to set such store by a glance! What a child a man is! We'd gone out to Walheim. The ladies drove, and during our walk I thought I saw, in Charlotte's dark eyes — I'm a fool, forgive me, but you should see them, those eyes! To keep it short, since my own eyes are heavy with sleep: well, the ladies were getting back into the carriage, and young W., Selstadt, Audran, and I stood about the door. They're a lively lot, all laughing and bantering. I watched Charlotte's eyes; they went from one to another — but not to me, to me, who stood there fixed on her and nothing else! My heart said goodbye to her a thousand times, and she never looked at me. The carriage rolled off, and tears stood in my eyes. I gazed after it — and then I saw Charlotte's bonnet lean out of the window, and she turned to look back. At me? Ah, dear friend, I can't be sure, and in that not knowing lies my comfort. Perhaps she turned for me. Perhaps! Goodnight. Oh, what a child I am!

The young man standing with the company as Charlotte’s carriage departs.
